~ i i i  AFP  Fonts:  Technical  Reference for Code  Pages

<<<PAGE 11>>>
This publication provides technical information about the code pages supplied 
with  IBM font offerings for use with IBM's  Advanced Function Presentation (AFP) 
licensed programs and page printers. 

IBM uses code pages to relate computer generated hexadecimal data to 
character shapes.  A  code page can be visualized as a 16 by  16 matrix of 
hexadecimal values, each value corresponding to an addressable unit called a 
code point.  Characters are assigned to code points according t o  conventions 
established by country needs and by various standards organizations, including 
IBM.  When a code point is selected, the character associated with that code 
point is printed or displayed.  For more information, see AFP  Fonts: Font 
Summary, 6544-3810, or AFP  Fonts: Font Summary for AFP Font  Collection, 
S544-5633. 

Code pages described in this publication are provided with several font offerings; 
however, the vast majority of the code pages presented here (all but six)  come 
with the AFP Font Collection products.  Code pages are used by many past and 
present font offerings including the IBM Expanded Core fonts (provided in AFP 
Font Collection), IBM Compatibility fonts (provided with the PSF  products), IBM 
4028  Font Metrics (no longer available), and other IBM font offerings, some of 
which are no longer available. 

For more information on the IBM Expanded Core fonts, see AFP  Fonts: Font 
Summary for AFP  Font  CoNection, S544-5633 or AFP Fonts: Technical Reference 
for Expanded  Core Fonts, 5544-5228. 

For more information on other font offerings,  see AFP  Fonts:  Font Summary, 
G544-3810. 

This publication should be  used by the system  programmer or the font 
administrator when working with IBM code pages.  This person should have a 
background in using AFP  applications in the MVS, OS/390, VM,  VSE,  AIX,  OS/2, 
or OS/400 operating system environments.  Knowledge of how fonts are used 
with IBM's  advanced function printers is also helpful. 

Contents of This  Publication 

This publication includes technical information about IBM code pages.  It 
includes the following chapters: 

Chapter  1,  "IBM  Fonts and Their Associated Code Pages" on page  1 
contains tables that summarize the code pages used with various language 
groups and IBM font products. 

Chapter  2,  "IBM Code  Pages and Their Language Groups" on page  17 
describes the categories in which IBM code pages are grouped, and includes 
a table that groups code pages by country. 

Chapter  3,  "IBM Code  Pages and Their Valid Character Sets"  on page  27 
contains lists of code pages showing the character sets and type families 
with which they can be  used.  Also contains a table listing the type families 
and their associated IBM character sets.

<<<PAGE 12>>>
Chapter  4,  "IBM  Code Pages" on  page  55 provides code page maps for 
each code page showing the code points and character assignments. 

Chapter  5,  "Character  IDS Found on IBM Code Pages" on page  301 lists 
each graphic character ID, the graphic character, the graphic character 
name, and the code pages on which you can find it.  Use this chapter 
whenever you need to find a code page to print a particular character. 

A glossary of terms and an index are also included. 

Related Publications 

The following publications contain additional information you  may find helpful 
when working with fonts and AFP  software. 

General 
Information 

AFP  Fonts: Font Summary, G544-3810, and AFP  Fonts: Font Summary for 
AFP  Font Collection, S544-5633, provide essential information about: 
- IBM Expanded Core Fonts 
- IBM Compatibility fonts

- IBM font licensed programs

Fonts: Font Summary, 6544-3810 was provided with IBM AF'P Font 
Collection 1.1  and AFP  Fonts: Font Summary for AFP  Font Collection , 
S544-5633  is provided with IBM AFP Font Collection 2.1.

describes type and how it benefits communication.

information about  how to select and use type in headings and text. 

AFP  Fonts: Font Samples, 6544-3792,  shows samples of fonts used with 
IBM's AFP programs and page printers.

This  publication has been updated 

To include code page information for code pages supporting the euro 
currency symbol added to the Latin1 fonts for IBM AFP  Font Collection 2.1.

Core fonts provided in IBM AFP Font Collection 2.1.

Core fonts provided in IBM AFP Font Collection 2.1.

properly identified.

edition.

Information Contained in This  Chapter 

This chapter summarizes fonts and their associated code pages.  If you  know the 
font you want to use, tables provided here can help you find a code page that 
can be used to print it. 

Figure  2 shows the IBM Expanded Core Font languages and their associated 
code pages.  The following shows how the these languages are supported by 
the Expanded Core type families. 
- APL:  Courier APL 
- Arabic: Times New Roman, Helvetica, and Courier 
- CyrillicIGreek: Times New Roman, Helvetica, and Courier 
- Hebtew: Times Mew  Roman, Helvetica, and Courier 
- Katakana: Gothic Kata kana 
- Lao: Times New Roman, Helvetica, and Courier 
- Latinl: Times New Roman, Helvetica, Courier, Boldface, Gothic Text, 
- Latin2-5: Times New Roman, Helvetica, and Courier 
- OCR: OCR-A  and OCR-B 
- Specials: BookMaster Specials and BookMaster IBM Logo 
- Symbols: Times New  Roman, Helvetica, Courier, and BookMaster 
- Thai: Times New Roman, Helvetica, and Courier 
Figure 3 shows IBM Compatibility fonts and their associated code pages. 

Letter Gothic, Prestige, and BookMaster 

Specials 

Figure '4 and Figure 5 show IBM 4028 Font Metrics and their associated code 
pages. 

Figure  6 shows IBM font licensed programs and their associated code 
pages. 

Chapter  3,  "IBM  Code Pages and Their Valid Character Sets" on  page  27 lists 
all valid character set and code page combinations (those character sets that 
contain all the characters required by a specific code page), regardless of 
whether a coded font exists for those combinations.  You can create coded fonts 
by using the information in AFP  Fonts: Technical Reference for IBM Expanded 
Core Fonts. 

Chapter 1.  IBM Fonts and Their Associated Code Pages  3

<<<PAGE 22>>>
IBM Expanded Core Languages and Their Associated Code Pages 

Figure  2 shows the language groups supported by the IBM Expanded Core Fonts 
and code pages that can be used to print them.  If a code page is shown for a 
language group (for example,  Latin4), then all type families supporting that 
language group (for example, Courier, Helvetica, and Times New Roman) can 
use the code page.  The only exceptions are BookMaster Latinl fonts which use 
a different set of code pages than the other  Latinl fonts. 

Note:  For a list of country-preferred  code pages (code pages preferred for use 
by a particular country), refer t o  "Code Pages for Specific Countries and 
Languages" on page  22 and AFP Fonts: Font Summary for AFP  Font Collection.

6  AFP Fonts:  Technical Reterence for Code Pages

<<<PAGE 25>>>
IBM 4028  Font Metrics and Their Associated Code Pages 

Note:  4028 Font Mstrics are no knger provided by IBM, but they are included In 

this puMition for reference purposes. 

IBM 4028 Font Metrics included uniformly spaced, mixed-pitch, and typographic 
fonts. 

Figure  4 shows code pages for uniformly spaced and mixed pitch 4028  Font 
Metrics. 
Figure 5 on page  1 1 shows code pages for typographic 4028  Font Metrics. 

The 4028  Font Metrics are the host equivalents of the printer-resident fonts.  To 
print on the IBM LaserPrinter 4028, you need to know if the coded font you are 
using requires that an IBM LaserPrinter 4028 font card be inserted into the 
printer.  For each coded font, Figure 4 and Figure  5 identify a font card that can 
be used.  The designation "Resident"  indicates that the font is internal to the 
printer and that a font card is not required. 

Note:  Not all possible font  cards are listed.  Refer to SupplieslOptions Catalog 

for the IBM LaserPrinter for more information about font cards.

provided for all code-page and character-set combinations.

Information Contained in This Chapter 

Often it is necessary to select a code page to print text for a particular country. 
This chapter explains how code pages can be identified by country or by 
language.  The concept of 'language  complement'  is explained and all supported 
countries and languages are identified along with code pages that  can used for 
printing. 

Preferred Code  Pages 

For many countries there are several code pages that, over time,  have become 
more or less standard code pages.  These are the preferred code pages and are 
a  good place to start when determining which code pages to use. 

Preferred code pages are all EBCDIC and are of four types: 

Country Extended Code Pages (CECP) 

Eum Country Extended Code  Pages (ECECP) 

BookMaster 

Country Specific 

Country Extended Code Pages are a collection of 14 Latinl code pages each 
containing the same characters, but with code point assignments arranged so 
that the characters are located according to the requirements of individual 
countries.  In other words, the same characters are supported by all 14 code 
pages, but the locations of the characters varies by code page.  These are the 
most widely-used code pages among countries with  Latin-based languages. 

Country Extended Code Pages are named TlVlOzu where zu  = 037,  273,  274, 
275,  277, 278,  280,  282,  284,285,  297,  500,  and 871.  TlBWlzzz  are the Country 
Extended Code Pages used with BookMaster. 

Euro Country Extended Code Pages are a subset of the Country Extended Code 
Pages and provide support for the euro currency symbol that was added to all 
Latinl fonts for AFP Font Collection 2.1.  The Country Extended Code  Pages were 
the logical choices for euro implementation; however, the code pages had no 
vacant code points available.  To address this problem, new Euro Country 
Extended Code  Pages were created based on 10 of the existing Country 
Extended Code  Pages.  The  'international  currency symbol',  SC010000,  was 
removed from these code pages and replaced by the euro sign.  Euro Country 
Extended Code Pages are named T100114n where n  = 0-9. 

Euro Country Extended Code Pages and their Country Extended counterparts  are 
listed below. 

ECECP TI001 140 based on CECP T I  V10037 
ECECP TI001 141 based on CECP TlV10273 
ECECP TI001 142 based on CECP TlVl0277 
ECECP Ti001 143 based on CECP TlV10278 
ECECP TI001144 based on CECP TlV10280 
ECECP T I  001 145 based on CECP TlV10284 
ECECP TI001 146 based on CECP TlV10285 
ECECP TI001 147 based on CECP TlV10297 
ECECP TI001 148 based on CECP TlV10500 

Chapter 2.  IBM Code Pages and Their Language Groups  19

<<<PAGE 38>>>
ECECP T100114Q based on CECP TlV10871 

BookMaster fonts and code pages have also had the euro sign added for AFP 
Font Collection 2.1.  Unlike the CECPs, however, it was not necessary to create 
new code pages because an unassigned code point, X'34',  was already 
available. 

Country Specific code pages are all other code pages that provide country 
support. 

Code Page Selection by  Language Complement 

The term 'language  complement'  was coined many years ago when the Core 
Interchange fonts were first introduced.  It is used to identify groups of countries 
that can be supported  by a single font.  For example, the Latinl language 
complement implies that text for any country  identified as a Latinl country can 
be printed with any  Latinl font.  Chapter 3 shows the fonts associated with the 
language complements. 

The following identifies the countries that are supported by each language 
complement. 

The Arabic  language complement contains characters for Latin and Arabic 
scripts. 
The CyrilliclGraek language complement contains characters for Bulgarian, 
Byelorussian, Macedonian, Russian, Serbian, Ukrainian, English, and Greek 
scripts. 
The Hebrew language complement contains characters for Latin and Hebrew 
scripts. 
The Latinl language complement contains characters for the following 
languages: Danish, Dutch, English, Faeroese, Finnish, French, German, 
Icelandic, Irish, Italian, Norwegian, Portuguese, Spanish, and Swedish. 
The Latin235 language complement contains characters for the following 
languages: 
-  Latin2:  Albanian,  Czech, English, German, Hungarian, Polish, Romanian, 
- Latin3; Afrikaans,  Catalan, Dutch. English, Esperanto, French, German, 
-  Latins: Danish, Dutch, English, Finnish, French, Irish, Italian, Norwegian, 

Serbocroatian, Slovak, and Slovenian. 

Italian, Maltese, Spanish, and Turkish. 

Portuguese, Spanish, Swedish, and Turkish. 

The Latin4 language complement contains characters for the following 
languages: Danish, English, Estonian, Finnish, French, German,  Greenlandic, 
Lappish, Latvian, Lithuanian, and Norwegian. 
The Thai language complement contains characters for  Latin and Thai 
scripts. 
The Lao language complement contains characters for Latin and Lao scripts. 
The Katakana language complement contains phonetic syllabic characters 
used for writing  non-Japanese words and for emphasis in Japanese text. 
The APL2  language complement contains characters used in the APL and 
APL2 programming languages. 
The Symbols language complement contains math and other special 
symbols. 
The OCR  language complements contain characters that support the OCR-A 
and OCR-B optical character recognition symbologies. 

20  AFP  Fonts:  Technical  Reference tor Code P a g e s

<<<PAGE 39>>>
Other EBCDIC Code Pages 

In addition to the  Preferred Code Pages, other EBCDIC code pages are 
sometimes available.  Generally, these are code pages that were associated 
with some of the old font licensed programs.  In most cases, they are simply 
alternatives to the preferred code pages. 

ASCll Code  Pages 

ASCll code pages are provided for cases where AFP documents must be printed 
in an ASCll environment.

Chapter 3.  IBM Code Pages and Their Valid Character Sets 

Figure  9 on page  30 lists the IBM-supplied code pages and shows the valid 
character sets that can be used with each code page.  All code pages in this 
book are provided with IBM AFP Font Collection  products except the following 6 
code pages: 

TI001132 and TI001133 come with MlCR Fonts for AFP, 5799-XDZ. 
TI001300 comes with Bar Code and OCR, 5688-021. 
T1001301, T1001302, and TI001303 come with  Postal Bar Code, 5799-DGX. 

Valid means that each character assigned in the code page is available in the 
character set. 

Character sets in this chapter are identified in two ways:  by category and by 
individual character set. 

Character Set Categories:  When  a category is identified, you can use all 
character sets in that category.  This includes all typefaces and point sizes.  For 
example, the category LATIN1 includes all Latin1 character sets for Times New 
Roman, Helvetica, Courier, Boldface, Gothic Text,  Letter Gothic, and Prestige in 
a wide variety of typeface and point size combinations. 

Figure  8 shows the character set categories.

Notes: 

1.  Figure  9 on page  30 lists the combinations of character sets and code pages 
that ISM has tried and found valid, but it is not an exhaustive list.  You  may 
find other combinations that work. 

2.  Character sets that begin with  "COE" are associated with the 4028 Font 

Metrics. 

In Print Services Facility (PSF)/MVS and PSF/VSE, the APSRMARK  utility 
identifies 4028  resident font resources to the host.  These font  resources 
include character sets and code pages.  Figure  9 includes all code pages for 

28  AFP  Fonts:  Technical Reference tor Code Pages 

I

<<<PAGE 47>>>
which 4028 Font Metrics are valid.  If you  use a code page for formatting text 
at the host, you  must ensure that the code  page is marked for  PSF/MVS or 
PSF/VSE  before trying to print text on the  IBM Laserprinter 4028.  For more 
information, refer to ABOUT  TYPE:  IBM's  Technical Reference for 4028 Font 
Metrics.

60  AFP  Fonts:  Technical  Reference for Code Pages

<<<PAGE 79>>>
Information Contained in This  Chapter 

A  code page provides the link between a computer generated code and a 
character in a font.  There are 256 code points in a code page into which 
character identifiers are stored.  When  a code point is selected, the character 
identifier assigned to that code point is used to locate the character shape  data 
in the font character set. 

The arrangement of characters on a code page is determined by country 
requirements and by registered standards. 

This chapter contains information about how to read a code page map, some 
special notes about code pages, and code pages for the IBM font products. 

How to Read a Code Page Map 

Each code page map includes detailed information and Figure  10 shows how this 
information is arranged.  Definitions of the terms  in Figure  10 follow the figure. 

I 

I 

Figure  10. Example of an IBM Code Page 

Code Page 
Descriptive Name 

The code page descriptive name describes the most common use of the code 
page.  For example, code page TlV10037 (Figure  10) contains the characters 
needed to print information in English.  It also includes special characters,  such 
as monetary symbols, used in Canada. 

Code Page ID 

The code page ID is the AFP identifier by which the code page is referenced. 

CPGlD 

GCSGID 

The CPGlD is the code page global ID.  It is a unique value that identifies a 
specific set of code points assigned t o  a specific list of graphic character  IDS. 

The GCSGID is the graphic character set global ID.  It is a unique value that 
identifies the list of graphic character IDS included in the code page. 

Chapter 4.  IBM Code Pages  61

<<<PAGE 80>>>
Encoding 

Default Character 

Most IBM code pages use EBCDIC code points; but some use ASCII.  Code 
pages that use ASCll code points cannot be processed by Document 
Composition Facility (DCF) because DCF does not support ASCll code pages. 
ASCll code pages, however, allow printing of ASCll data streams that have 
been fonatted by other means. 

When the printer receives a code point that does not have a character 
assigned to it, and data checks are prevented in either of these ways: 

The printer data check is blocked (you specified  DATACK - BLOCK to PSF, 
The code page specifies  DATA CHECK - NO, 

or BLKCHAR in job  control) 

the default character is printed.  If the data check is not blocked and the code 

page you are using specifies DATA CHECK - YES, the printer issues a data 
When DATA CHECK - YES,  some printers perform extensive recovery for invalid 

check.  Code pages received from IBM normally specify DATA CHECK  -- YES. 

characters even if data checks are blocked to PSF, at great cost to 
performance.  You  can improve performance by using the Type Transformer 
code page editor to set DATA CHECK  = NO, but you will not be warned  about 
undefined code points.  You will not know that the characters you expect were 
not printed until you proofread the printout. 

Code Point 

All the code pages in this book specify a  blank (character ID SW10000) as the 
default character for undefined code points. 

For information about editing code pages, see AFP  Fonts: Type Transformer 
Usef s Guide. 

A  1-byte code is assigned to each character in the code page.  The code points 
are represented as hexadecimal values;  for example, hexadecimal 50 is 
equivalent to decimal 80.  The numeral o r  letter above the character in the 
matrix is the first hexadecimal digit of the code point.  The numeral or letter to 
the left of the character is the second hexadecimal digit.  In Figure  10 on 
page  61,  for example, character ID SM030000 (&) is assigned to hexadecimal 
code point 50. 

Character Graphic 

Each character that appears on the code page is associated with its character 
ID. 

Character ID 

The character ID is the standard identifier for a character regardless of its type 
family.  For example, all uppercase A's  have the same character ID (LA020000). 
Character IDS are also called graphic character identifiers or graphic character 
IDS. 

Baseline 

The baseline shows where the character is aligned  in a line of text. 

Changing Code Pages 

It is possible to add,  delete, and change code point assignments for characters. 
One technique is to use the code page editor provided with Type Transformer. 
For instructions, see AFP  Fonts: Type Transfonner'Usef s  Guide. 

62  AFP  Fonts:  Technical Reference for Code  Pages

<<<PAGE 81>>>
Identifying Similar Code Pages 

Two code pages  may appear to be identical;  however, they are not identical 
unless both the code page global identifier (CPGID) and graphic character set 
global identifier (GCSGID) on both code pages are the same.  Figure  10 shows 
where the CPGID and GCSGID are located on IBM code pages.  Figure  11 shows 
two similar code pages. 

Figure  1 I .   Example of  Two Similar IBM Code Pages 

Notice that code  pages TIDOBASE and TI001002 are similar but not identical. 
Code page TIDOBASE has a CPGID of  2063 and no GCSGID.  Code page 
TIDOBASE has braces assigned to two sets of code points: 

X18B1 and X19B1 
XICO'  and X'DO1 

Code page TI001002 has a CPGID of 1002 and a GCSGID of 1132.  Code page 
TI001002 has braces assigned to only one set of code points:  X18B' and X19B'. 

In addition, the three variable  space characters that were used by DCF 
(SP010001, SW10002, and SP010003) are available on code page TIDOBASE but 
not on code page T1001002. 

Chapter 4.  IBM Code Pages  63

<<<PAGE 82>>>
---- 

Code Pages 

The code  pages in this chapter are arranged  by code page ID according to the 
standard EBCDIC collating sequence: A to 2, then 0 to 9. 

64  AFP  Fonts:  Technical Reference for Code  Pages

<<<PAGE 321>>>
Every character has a unique identifier.  Once you understand the naming 
conventions used for character IDS (graphic character identifiers), you can 
recognize the characters included in code pages and character  sets. 

This chapter lists the character IDS and the code pages on which you can find 
them.  Once you know which code pages contain the characters you need, you 
can go to Chapter 3 to see which fonts contain them. 

Occasionally, characters are provided in font data that are not directly 
associated with a code page.  You  can  use the code page editor supplied with 
Type Transformer in AFP Font Collection to add these characters to a code page. 
For information about editing code pages, see AFP Fonts:  Type Transformer 
Usef s Guide. 

IBM Graphic Character Identifier Order and Prefixes 

The graphic character IDS are listed in order according to the standard EBCDIC 
collating sequence, that is, A-Z,  then 0-9,  This order does not correspond to 
the actual alphabetical order for some languages. 

Figure  12 identifies the order in which categories of graphic character IDS are 
presented and the meaning of the prefix in each graphic character ID.

Letters for the Arabic alphabet.  The second character 
generally correlates to the Latin alphabet, for example, 
AA  for Arabic A. 
Letters for the Thai alphabet.  The second character 
generally correlates to the Latin alphabet, for example, 
BA  for Thai A. 
Letters for the Lao alphabet. 
Uppercase and lowercase letters for the Greek alphabet. 
The second character generally correlates to the Latin 
alphabet, for example, GA  for uppercase or  lowercase 
Greek A. 
Letters for the Hebrew alphabet.  The second character 
generally correlates to the Latin alphabet, for example, 
HX  for  Hebrew X 
Japanese phonetics, such as 7 (HU, JH300000), 3 (KO, 
JK500000), and X  (NU,  JN300000). 
Uppercase and lowercase letters for the Cyrillic 
languages.  The second character generally correlates to 
the Latin alphabet, for example,  KA for uppercase or 
lowercase Cyrillic A. 
Uppercase and lowercase letters for the Romance 
languages.  The second character indicates the 
unaccented letter of the alphabet, for example.  LA for 
uppercase or lowercase Latin A.

Glossary 

This glossary includes definitions from the following 
sources: 

Definitions reprinted from the American National 
Dictionary for Information Processing Systems are 
identified by the symbol (A) following the 
definition. 

Definitions reprinted from a published section of 
the International Organization for 
Standardization's Vocabulary-Information 
Processing or from a published section of the IS0 
Vocabulary-Offlce  Machines are identified by the 
symbol (I) following the definition.  Because many 
IS0 definitions are also reproduced in the 
American National Dictionary for Information 
Processing Systems,  IS0 definitions may also be 
identified by the symbol (A). 

Definitions reprinted from working documents, 
draft proposals, or draft international standards of 
IS0 Technical Committee 97,  Subcommittee 1 
(Vocabulary) are identified by the symbol (T) 
following the definition, indicating that final 
agreement has not yet been reached among its 
participating members. 
Definitions that are specific t o  IBM products are 
so labeled, for example, "In SNA,"  or  "In VM." 

References 

The following cross references are used in this 
glossary: 

Contrast with.  This refers to a term that has an 
opposed or substantively different meaning. 

Synonym for.  This indicates that the term has the 
same meaning as a preferred term, which is defined 
in its proper place in the dictionary. 

Synonymous with.  This is a backward reference from 
a defined term to all other terms that have the same 
meaning. 

See.  This refers the reader to multiple-word terms 
that have the same last word. 

See a h .   This refers the reader to terms that have a 
related, but not synonymous, meaning. 

Advanced  Function Printing (AFP).  The ability of 
programs to print all-points-addressaMe text and 
images. 

Advanced Function Printing data stream (AFPDS). 
The printer data stream used for  printing Advanced 

Function Printing data.  The AFPDS  includes 
composed text, page segments, electronic overlays, 
form definitions, and fonts that are downloaded from 
the operating system to the printer. 

AFM.  Type 1 font files that contain font metrics 
(character width and kerning information).  Contrast 
with PFA  and PFB. 

AFP.  Advanced Function Printing. 

AFPDS.  Advanced Function Printing data stream. 

all points addressable (APA).  (1 )  In computer 
graphics, pertaining to the ability to address and 
display or not display each picture element (pel) on ;I 
display surface.  (2)  See also picture element. 

alphabetic character.  (1) A letter or other symbol, 
excluding numerals, used in a language.  (2) Any one 
of the letters A through Z (uppercase and lowercase). 
Some licensed programs include as alphabetic 
characters the special characters #, S,  and @. 

alphameric.  Synonym for alphanumeric. 

alphanumeric.  Pertaining to a character set that 
contains letters, numerals, and usually other 
characters,  such as punctuation marks. (1) 
Synonymous with alphameric. 

American National Standards Institute (ANSI).  An 
organization, consisting of producers, consumers, a n l  
general interest groups, that establishes the 
procedures by which accredited organizations create 
and maintain voluntary industry standards in the 
United States. (A) 

ANSI.  American National Standards Institute. 

APA.  All points addressable. 

=rider.  (1 )  In a font, the distance from the 
baseline to the top of the character box.  This value 
can differ for different characters of a given font. 
(2)  Contrast with descender. 

ASCII (American National Standard Code for 
lnfonnaion Interchange).  The standard code, using ,a 
coded character set consisting of 7-bit  coded 
characters (8-bits inciuding parity check), that is usecl 
for information interchange among data processing 
systems,  data communication systems, and 
associated equipment.  The ASCII set consists of 
control characters and graphic characters. (A) 

A-space.  The area in the character box to the left of 
the first picture element (pel) of the graphic character 
raster pattern.  See also B-space  and C-space. 

Glossary  447

<<<PAGE 466>>>
attribute.  (1)  A named property of an entity.  (2) A 
property of a character set, for example, weight, 
width, or style.  (3) See also character attribute. 

&.space.  The area in the character box that has a 
pel defined for  any row of the graphic character 
pattern.  See also A-space and C-space. 

bar code.  A code representing characters by sets of 
parallel bars of varying thickness and separation, that 
are read optically by transverse scanning. (1) 

baseline.  (1) The primary horizontal reference line 
for character alignment and measurement of vertical 
distances.  (T)  (2)  In a font, the imaginary line on 
which the bottom of each successive character is 
aligned. 

baseline direction.  (1) The direction in which 
successive lines of text are added.  (2)  Synonymous 
with print direction. 

baoeline offset.  Synonym for ascender. 

baseline position.  The location down from the top of 
the logical page (the uppermost point that can be 
printed on the page) at which the first line of text 
should start.  The baseline position varies from 
character to character.  The values given in this 
publication are the maximum baseline offset values 
found in the O0  Font Orientation (FNO) structured field. 

bit map.  A coded representation in which each bit, or 
group of bits, represents or corresponds to an item. 
For example, a configuration of bits in main storage in 
which each bit indicates whether  a peripheral device 
or storage block is available or in which each group of 
bits corresponds to on pixel 
of a dispaly image. 

bold.  (1)  Any typeface characterized by wide 
strokes.  (2) See also semibold.  (3) Contrast with 
light. 

boldface.  (1) A heavy-faced type.  (2)  Printing in 
heavy-faced type. 

bounded-box format.  (1) An organization of 
character graphics and'information used by Advanced 
Function Printing programs for printing on printers 
such as the IBM 3900 Advanced Function Printer or 
IBM Laserprinter 4028.  Character boxes containing 
each character graphic do not require character 
positioning information in the form of white space in 
the character boxes.  In addition, a single character 
set can be used for  all combinations of character 
rotation and text orientation.  Fonts in bounded-box 
format usually require less storage than fonts in 
unbounded-box  format.  (2) See also unbounded-box 
format. 

bounded-box relative rnetrics.  (1 )  Units of 
measurement that are expressed in 1000 units per 
Em-square.  (2)  See also relative metrics. 

448  AFP  Fonts:  Technical Referena for Code Pages 

byte.  (1) A binary character operated upon as a unit 
and usually shorter than a computer word.  (A)  (2) A 
string that consists of a particular number of bits, 
usually 8,  that is treated as a unit,  and that 
represents a  character. 

caps.  (1)  Capital letters, an uppercase font.  (2) A 
printing style that uses two type sizes of a single 
uppercase font.  The smaller size is used instead of a 
lowercase font.  See also small caps. 

character.  (1) A member of a set of elements that is 
used for the representation, organization, or control of 
data.  (T)  (2) A letter, numeral, or other symbol that 
is used as part of the organization, control, or 
representation of data.  A character is often in the 
form of a spatial arrangement of adjacent or 
connected strokes.  (A) 

character attribute.  (1)  In the 3270 data stream, a 
code that defines a single property of a character or 
characters, for example, extended color, character 
set, or extended highlighting.  A character can have 
more than one defined character attribute.  (2)  See 
also attribute. 

character baseline.  An imaginary line on which 
characters are aligned in the text formatting process. 

character box.  (1 )  An imaginary parallelogram on a 
display surface that contains all parts of one graphic 
character. (T) (2) The maximum area in which a 
symbol and all associated elements, such as a cursor, 
an underline, or space surrounding the symbol to 
separate it from other symbols, can be printed or 
displayed. 

character graphic.  The visual representation of a 
character, defined by toned or untoned picture 
elements (pels).  An untoned pel (a reverse character) 
is visually represented by the toned pels around it. 

character ID.  Character identifier. 

character identifier.  (1) A  symbol that represents a 
character.  (2)  The identifier that represents a 
character, regardless of its style, for example, all 
uppercase A's  have the same character ID. 
(3)  Synonymous with graphic character identifier. 

character increment.  (1 )  The distance the current 
print position is increased for the particular character 
printed.  (2) Synonymous with character spacing. 

character Ikt.  Synonym for complement.

<<<PAGE 467>>>
character metrics.  (1 )  Measurement information that 
defines individual character values such as height, 
width,  and space.  Character metrics can be 
expressed in specific fixed units, such as pels, or in 
relative units that are independent of both the 
resolution and size of the font.  Metrics are often 
included as part of the more general term font 
metrics.  (2) See also font metrics. 

character properties.  Details about how a character 
is printed relative to the other characters around it. 
Character properties are box size,  horizontal and 
vertical character size, character ID, center line, 
baseline, left space (A-space), right space (Cspace), 
above space,  and below space. 

character raster pattern.  The scan patterns for a 
graphic character of a particular size, weight, and 
style. 

chamtar rotation.  In printing, the alignment of a 
character with respect to the baseline, measured in 
degrees in a clockwise direction.  Examples are 0°, 
90°,  180°, and 270'.  Z d e g r e e  character rotation 
exists when a character is in its customary alignment 
with the baseline. 

character set.  (1)  A finite set of different characters 
that is complete for a given purpose, for example, the 
character set in IS0 Standard 646, "7-Bit  Coded 
Character Set for Information Processing 
Interchange."  (1) (2) A group of characters used for 
a specific reason, for example, the set of characters a 
printer can print.  (3)  Often a synonym for font 
character set.  (4) See also.coded font 

character spacing.  Synonym for character increment. 

coded font.  (1)  A font library member that 
associates a code page and a font character set. 
(2)  See also character set, code page, and font 
character set 

code page.  (1) A font library member that associates 
code points and character identifiers.  A code page 
also identifies invalid code points.  (2)  See also coded 
font 

code page g b h l  identifier (CPGID). 
registered identifier used to specify a particular 
assignment of code points to graphic characters. 
(2)  See also graphic character set global identifier. 

(1) A Sdigit 

code point.  A 1-byte code representing one of 
2%  potential characters. 

Compatibility Font.  Synonym for IBMSupplied 
Compatibility Font 

complement.  (1) A collection of graphic character 
identifiers.  For IBM Expanded Core Fonts, 
complements are language-oriented.  For example, 
the Cyrillic Greek complement contains Cyrillic, 

Greek,  and Latin alphabetic characters. 
(2)  Synonymous with character list. 

condensed type.  A typeface in which all characters 
are narrowed, making them appear taller.  Contrast 
with expanded type. 

copyfitting.  The process of deciding how much space 
handwritten or typed copy will occupy when using 
proportionally spaced type. 

country extended code page.  An &bit code page that 
has a 93character set on its nationally standardized 
code points but is extended to the multilingual 
character set for the national languages of some 
European countries. 

CPGID.  Code page global identifier. 

C-space.  The area in the character box to the right 
of the rightmost picture element for the graphic 
character pattern.  See also A-space  and B-space. 

cursive.  (1)  Letters created to imitate handwriting. 
(2)  Synonym for script. 

wstom font.  A font designed or modified by a 
customer. 

cylinder.  (1)  In an  assembly of magnetic disks, the 
set of all tracks that can be accessed by all the 
magnetic heads of a comb in a given position. 
(2)  The tracks of a disk storage device that can be 
accessed without repositioning the access 
mechanism. 

DASD.  Direct access storage device. 

DCF.  Document Composition Facility. 

default.  Pertaining to an attribute, value, or option 
that is assumed when  none is specified.  (I) 

descender.  (1)  In a font, the distance from the 
baseline to the bottom of the character box.  This 
value can differ for different characters of a given 
font.  (2)  Contrast with ascender. 

diacritic.  A mark added to a letter to indicate a 
special phonetic value. 

direct access storage device (DASD).  A device in 
which access time is effectively independent of the 
location of the data. 

document.  (1)  A named, structural unit of text that 
can be stored, retrieved, and exchanged among 
systems and users as a separate unit. (1) 
(2)  Information and the medium on which it is 
recorded that can be read by humans or by machine. 
(3)  In word processing, synonymous with file.

<<<PAGE 468>>>
Document Camposltiin Facility (DCF).  A licensed 
program that provides text formatting for a number of 
IBM printers. 

individual or collections of graphic characters. 
(2)  See also font metrics.  (3)  Contrast with relative 
metrics. 

EBCDIC.  Extended binary-coded decimal interchange 
code.  A coded character set consisting of &bit coded 
characters. (A) 

In printing, a unit of measure equal to the side 

em. 
of  a square formed by the font point size.  In relative 
metrics, the em is always 1000 relative units for 
medium-width fonts and is scaled up or down for 
expanded and condensed fonts. 

em space.  See em. 

In printing, a unit of measure equal to one-half

the width of an em.  For many typefaces, lowercase

characters.  (2)  Often synonymous with character set.

escapement.  (1 )  Movement of one character space 
between the paper carrier and typing or printing 
position, parallel with the typing or printing line. 
(2) The unit of vertical or  horizontal movement that is 
built into a device, for example, the 1403 printer with 
a 10-pitch train has a horizontal escapement unit of 
l/lOth inch.

font component.  One of the three basic parts that

identifies the character style and size for certain 
printers.  (2) The unique value that identifies the type

Examples of  attributes used to form a descriptive font

expressed in specified fixed units, such as pels, or in

resolution and size of the font.  (2)  See also character 
metrics, fixed rnetrics, and relative metrics.

IBM 4028 Font Metrics. 
found in the IBM LaserPrinter 4028.  This information 
includes font descriptions.  IBM 4028  Font Metrics

characters themselves.  Therefore the 4028  Font

Uniformly spaced fonts are measured in pitch.

approximately 12 points in a pica.  (2)  Contrast with

printer.  (1 )  An output unit that produces a hard copy

writes output data fmm a system on paper or other

IBM Laserprinter 4028 font card.

Print Management Facility (PMF).  An interactive, 
menu-driven program that can be used to create and 
modify fonts and to define output formatting for data 
printed on a page printer.

supports advanced function printers.  PSF can 
interface either directly to a user's application 
program or indirectly through the Job Entry 
Subsystems of MVS. 

program.  A sequence of instructions suitable for 
processing by  a computer.  Processing can include 
the use of an assembler, a compiler, an interpreter, or 
a translator to prepare the program for execution, as 
well as to execute it. (I) 

program library.  (1)  An organized collection of 
computer programs, or parts of computer programs, 
and possibly information pertaining to their use.  A 
program library is often called according to the 
characteristic of its elements, for example, a 
procedure library, a source program library. 
(2)  Synonym for  partitioned data set 

proportion.  Relationship of the width of a letter to its 
height 

proportionally spaced font.  (1) A font with graphic 
characters contained in character cells varying with 
the size of each graphic character.  This allows for 
even spacing between printed characters and 
eliminates excess white space around narrow 
characters, such as the letter  i. (2)  Contrast with 
uniformly spaced font. 

proportional spacing.  Spacing of characters 
according to their natural width.  Contrast with 
monospacing. 

PSF.  Print Services Facility. 

M e r .   (1)  In computer graphics, a predetermined 
pattem of lines that provides uniform coverage of a 
display space.  (2)  In advanced function printers, an 
onloff pattern of electrostatic images produced by the 
laser print head under control of the character 
generator. 

454  AFP  Fonts:  Technical Reference for Code Pages 

raater pattern.  (1 )  A  series of picture elements (pels) 
arranged in scan lines to form an image.  (2) See also 
picture element. 

relative metrics.  (1 )  Measurements in dimensionless 
units for one or several graphic characters. 
Dimensionless units are expressed as fractional parts 
of a unit-square design space (em-square) whose 
sides correspond to the vertical size of the font. 
Because the units are dimensionless, the same 
metrics can be used for different point sizes and 
different raster-pattern resolutions.  Relative metrics 
require defining the units of measure for the 
em-square, the point size of the font,  and,  if 
applicable, the pel resolution of the raster pattern. 
For example, for 1000 units per em-square, a relative 
value of 500  (500/1000 of an em-square) is equivalent 
to 0.08333  inches for a 12-point 240-pel font (500/1000 
x 12i72 inch) or 20  pels (500/1000 x 240 x  12/72 inch). 
(2)  See also bounded-box (3)  Contrast with fixed 
metrics.  relative metrics and font metrics. 

resolution.  (1 )  In computer graphics, a measure of 
the sharpness of an image, expressed as the number 
of lines and columns on the display screen or  the 
number of pels per unit area.  (2)  See also pel 
density. 

reverse image.  Converting images so that Mack 
becomes white and white becomes black 

roman.  Relating to a type style with upright letters. 

rotation.  (1)  In printing, the number of degrees a 
graphic character is turned relative to the page 
coordinates.  (2)  See also orientation. 

sans ser%.  (1) Type that is characterized by the 
absence of  serifs.  (2)  Contrast with serif. 

script.  (1 )  Letters created to imitate handwriting. 
(2) Synonymous with cursive. 

semibold.  (1) A typeface that is lighter than bold but 
darker than medium.  (2) See also bold. 

serif.  (1) A short line angling from or crossing the 
free end of a stroke.  This includes horizontal lines at 
the top and bottom of vertical strokes on capital 
letters, for example,  I and H,  and the decorative 
stroke at the end of one or more of the horizontal 
members, for example, a capital  E.  (2)  Contrast with 
sans serif. 

small caps.  Capital letters in the same style as the 
normal capital letters in a font,  but approximately the 
size of the lowercase letters.  See also caps.

software.  All or part of the programs, procedures, 
rules, and associated documentation of a data 
processing system.  Software is an intellectual 
creation that is independent of the medium on which 
it is recorded. (T)  Contrast with hardware. 

space.  (1) A  basic unit of area, usually the size of a 
single character.  (A)  (2) A blank area separating 
words or lines.  (3)  To cause a printer to move the 
paper a specified number of lines either before or 
after it prints a line. 

special character.  (1) A graphic character that is not 
a letter, numeral, or blank character,  and usually not 
an ideogram, for example, a punctuation mark, 
general currency symbol, or percent sign.  (T)  (2) A 
character other than a numeral, letter, #, $,  or @,  for 
example, *,  +, and % are special characters.

. 

style. 
Inclination of a letter around a vertical axis.  It

or  leave. (T) 

text.  In text processing, a sequence of elements 
intended to convey a meaning, whose interpretation is 
essentially based upon the reader's knowledge of 
some natural language or artificial language, for 
example, a business letter printed on paper or 
displayed on a screen.  The elements can consist of 
characters, symbols, words,  phrases, paragraphs, 
sentences, or tables. (T)

translating.  The process of incorporating characters

Type 1 font.  A font conforms to the format describecl 
in the publication Adobe Type  I  Font Format, 
available from Adobe Systems, Incorporated.  Type 1 
fonts are also known as outline fonts, from which 
fonts of different sizes can be generated.  Type 1 
fonts are generally shipped with two files: an .AFM  file 
and a .PFB file. 

a 

Type 1 name.  A name which is an alphabetic 
description of a character.  For example, aacute 
describes a lowercase a with a acute accent. 

typekce.  (1)  A specific type style, such as Helveticsi 
or Times New  Roman.  (2) One of the many attribute:; 
of a font, others, for example, being size and weight. 
(3) A collection of fonts, each having a different 
height or  size of character sets.

rwardless of attributes, such as width, weight, style, 
and size,  for example, Courier or  Helvetica. 

type font.  Type of a given size and style, for 
example,  1 0-point  Latin1 : Helvetica roman medium. 
(A)  See also font. 

typeset.  (1) To arrange the type on a page for 
printing.  (2)  Pertaining to material that has been set 
in type. 

type site.  (1) A measurement in pitch or points of 
the height and width of a graphic character in a font. 
(2) One of the many attributes of a font, others, for 
example, being weight and typeface. 

type styk.  The form of characters within a set of the: 
same font, for example, elite and pica. (T)  Attributes 
such as posture, style, weight,  and width can vary in a 
type style. 

type weight.  (1) The degree of boldness of a 
typeface series, caused by different thickness of the 
strokes that form a graphic character.  (2)  One of the 
many attributes of a font, others, for example, being 
size and typeface.  (3) The weight can be described i m  
such terms as light, semibold,  bold, and so on. 

type wktth.  The horizontal size (set size) of a given 
typeface.  The width can be given in units of 
measurement, such as  set 9 point, or  it can be 
descriptive: ultra-condensed or expanded.

-  storage than fonts in bounded-box format.  (2)  See
